Lecture notes on flow equation approach to singular stochastic PDEs
Paweł Duch

Abstract
The flow equation approach is a robust framework applicable to a broad class of singular SPDEs, including those with fractional Laplacians, throughout the entire subcritical regime. Inspired by Wilson’s renormalization group, this method studies the coarse-grained process, which captures the behaviour of solutions across spatial scales. The corresponding flow equation describes how the nonlinear terms in the effective dynamics evolve with the coarse-graining scale, playing a role analogous to the Polchinski equation in quantum field theory. The renormalization problem is then solved inductively by imposing appropriate boundary conditions on the flow equation. MSC classification: 60H17, 81T17



Contents

1
Singular subcritical SPDEs

Stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs) arise naturally in the mathematical description of systems subject to random influences, such as fluctuating interfaces, turbulent fluids, or fields in statistical mechanics and quantum field theory. They provide a framework for studying the interplay between deterministic dynamics and randomness, often serving as continuum limits of large stochastic systems or as models of fluctuating physical quantities at small scales.

While many SPDEs can be treated using classical analytic techniques, a particularly challenging class, known as singular SPDEs, has emerged as a central object of modern probability theory and mathematical physics. These equations are
singular
in the sense that their solutions are too irregular for the nonlinear terms appearing in the equations to be well-defined in the classical sense. In particular, the driving noises (such as the space-time white noise) are so rough that even the notion of a product of the solution with itself may not make sense. This issue mirrors the ultraviolet divergences encountered in quantum field theory, where renormalization techniques are required to define interacting models.

A prominent motivation for studying singular SPDEs comes from stochastic quantization, a program introduced by Parisi and Wu
[
PW81
]
in the early 1980s to construct quantum field theories as invariant measures of stochastic dynamics. The corresponding evolution equations—such as the dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{d}
and Sine-Gordon models—are prototypical examples of singular SPDEs. These models also serve as paradigms for understanding universality classes of stochastic systems, including the celebrated Kardar–Parisi–Zhang (KPZ) equation, which describes random interface growth.

Over the last decade, several groundbreaking theories have been developed to give rigorous meaning to singular SPDEs and to construct their solutions in a systematic way. Among these, the theory of regularity structures introduced by Hairer
[
Hai14
]
, and the paracontrolled distributions approach developed by Gubinelli, Imkeller, and Perkowski
[
GIP15
]
, stand as two landmark frameworks. Both methods combine analytic and probabilistic ideas to extend the scope of classical PDE theory into the singular regime.

The present notes focus on an approach to singular SPDEs grounded in the renormalization group (RG) method. Originating from Wilson’s seminal ideas
[
Wil71
]
, this framework analyzes how the effective description of an SPDE transforms across scales, determining which terms require renormalization to obtain a mathematically consistent solution theory. The RG perspective on singular SPDEs was first rigorously developed by Kupiainen
[
Kup16
,
KM17
]
, who employed a discrete RG scheme to construct solutions to the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model and the KPZ equation. The flow equation formalism
[
Duc25a
,
Duc25b
]
provides a continuous-scale counterpart of this philosophy, yielding a unified and systematic approach to singular SPDEs throughout the full subcritical regime.

In these notes, we restrict attention to equations driven by white noise (or its periodic version). Recall that the white noise
\xi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the centered Gaussian random distribution taking values in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and characterized by

\mathbb{E}(\langle\xi,f\rangle\langle\xi,g\rangle)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(x)g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

for all
f,g\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We now list a few canonical examples of singular SPDEs that arise in this setting:

(1)

Dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model, also known as the parabolic stochastic quantization equation of the
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model, with
d\in\{2,3\}
– a parabolic SPDE of the form

(\partial_{t}+1-\Delta)\varPhi=\xi-\lambda\,\varPhi^{3}+\infty\,\varPhi

(1.1)

posed in spacetime
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
driven by the spacetime white noise
\xi
.

(2)

Dynamical fractional
\Phi^{4}_{d,\sigma}
model with
d\in\{2,3,4\}
and
\sigma\in(d/2,d]
– a parabolic non-local SPDE of the form

(\partial_{t}+1+(-\Delta)^{\sigma/2})\varPhi=\xi-\lambda\,\varPhi^{3}+\infty\,\varPhi

(1.2)

posed in spacetime
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
driven by the spacetime white noise
\xi
and involving the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}
of order
\sigma
. Note that for
\sigma=2
the above SPDE coincides with the standard dynamical
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model. Formally, the
\Phi^{4}_{d,\sigma}
measure

\mu(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\exp\left(-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\phi(x)\big(1+(-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}\big)\phi(x)+\lambda\,\phi(x)^{4}/2-\infty\,\phi(x)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi,

is invariant for the above SPDE, i.e. if
\varPhi
is a solution of the above SPDE with the initial condition
\varPhi(0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})=\phi
distributed according to the above measure, then
\varPhi(t,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})
is also distributed according to this measure for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

(3)

Elliptic stochastic quantization equation of
\Phi^{4}_{d}
model with
d\in\{2,3\}
– an elliptic SPDE of the form

(1-\Delta)\varPhi=\xi-\lambda\,\varPhi^{3}+\infty\,\varPhi

(1.3)

posed in space
\mathbb{R}^{2+d}
driven by the white noise
\xi
. Formally, the solution of the above equation evaluated at the hyperspace of co-dimension two
\varPhi(0,0,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})
is distributed according to the
\Phi^{4}_{d,\sigma=2}
measure.

(4)

Dynamical Sine-Gordon model with
\beta\in(0,\sqrt{8\pi})
– a parabolic SPDE of the form

(\partial_{t}+1-\Delta)\varPhi=\xi-\lambda\,\infty\,\beta\,\sin(\beta\varPhi)

(1.4)

posed in spacetime
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
driven by the spacetime white noise
\xi
. Formally, the measure

\mu(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}}\exp\left(-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Big(\phi(x)(1-\Delta)\phi(x)+2\lambda\,\infty\,\cos(\beta\phi(x))\Big)\mathrm{d}x\right)\,\mathrm{d}\phi

is invariant for the above SPDE.

All of the above SPDEs are singular whenever
\lambda\neq 0
. In the linear case
\lambda=0
, the equations can be solved explicitly, but the resulting solution is a random Schwartz distribution rather than a genuine function. This simple observation already signals the central difficulty: for
\lambda\neq 0
, we must look for solutions in a space of distributions. However, since multiplication of distributions is in general ill-defined, the nonlinear terms appearing in these equations lack an a priori meaning.

The standard strategy to overcome this problem is to introduce an ultraviolet (UV) regularization—that is, to smooth out the noise or the Green’s function at small spatial scales. One then aims to show that, after performing suitable renormalizations of the nonlinear terms, a well-defined and nontrivial limit exists as the regularization is removed. The renormalization procedure compensates for the divergences arising at small scales by subtracting divergent constants (formally denoted above by “infinite” quantities), thereby ensuring that the limiting solution remains finite and mathematically meaningful.

In these notes, we focus on a specific example of such a singular equation, namely the elliptic SPDE

(1-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}\varPhi=\xi+\lambda\,\varPhi^{3}-\infty\,\varPhi,

(1.5)

posed on the space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and driven by the
2\pi
-periodization of white noise
\xi
. For concreteness, we take
d=5
and
\sigma\in(d/3,d/2]
, which corresponds to the full subcritical regime. This range ensures that, while the equation remains singular, the renormalized theory is expected to be mathematically well-posed. Although the elliptic equation (
1.5
) is not motivated by direct physical applications, it serves as a clean and instructive testing ground for the renormalization group (RG) approach. The methods developed here extend, at least in principle, to a wide range of SPDEs introduced earlier.

To give meaning to (
1.5
), we first regularize the driving noise. Let
\vartheta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be an even, nonnegative function supported in the unit ball and normalized so that
\int\vartheta=1
. For
\kappa\in[0,1]
, define the rescaled mollifier
\vartheta_{\kappa}(x):=[\kappa]^{-d}\vartheta(x/[\kappa])
, where
[\kappa]:=\kappa^{1/\sigma}
. The function
\vartheta_{\kappa}
is supported in a ball of radius
[\kappa]
and converges to the Dirac delta as
\kappa\searrow 0
. For
\kappa\in(0,1]
. we define the regularized noise by
\xi_{\kappa}:=\vartheta_{\kappa}\ast\xi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\ast
denotes the convolution. We have
\xi_{\kappa}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
\kappa\in(0,1]
and
\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\xi_{\kappa}=\xi\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
almost surely. We rewrite the singular SPDE (
1.5
) in the following regularized mild form

\varPhi=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi],\qquad\kappa\in(0,1],

(1.6)

where
G\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the fundamental solution for the pseudo-differential operator
(1-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}
. The functional
F_{\kappa}[\varphi]
, called the force, is defined by

F_{\kappa}[\varphi](x):=\xi_{\kappa}(x)+\lambda\varphi^{3}(x)+\sum_{i=1}^{i_{\sharp}}\lambda^{i}c_{\kappa}^{(i)}\varphi(x),

(1.7)

where
i_{\sharp}:=\lfloor\sigma/(3\sigma-d)\rfloor
and the parameters
c_{\kappa}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}
depending on the UV cutoff
\kappa
are called the counterterms. These constants are introduced to cancel the divergences arising from the nonlinear interaction in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
. The number of such counterterms required to renormalize the cubic nonlinearity increases without bound as
\sigma\searrow d/3
. Consequently, the renormalization problem becomes progressively more intricate near the critical threshold
\sigma=d/3
, where the equation ceases to be subcritical.

We can now formulate the main result of these notes, which establishes that, after suitable renormalization, the family of regularized equations (
1.6
) admits a well-defined and nontrivial limit as
\kappa\searrow 0
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d\in\{1,\ldots,6\}
and
\sigma\in(d/3,d/2]
. There exist a choice of counterterms and random variables
\lambda_{\star}\in[0,1]
and
\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that:
(0) for every random variable
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{\star},\lambda_{\star}]
and
\kappa\in(0,1]
(
1.6
) has a solution
\varPhi_{\kappa}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
(1) it holds
\varPhi_{0}=\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\kappa}
almost surely in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
(2) it holds
\mathbb{E}(\lambda_{\star}^{-n})<\infty
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

Remark 1.2
.

Note that
\lambda
, the prefactor of the non-linear term in the equation, is assumed to be random and sufficiently small. In the case of parabolic equations the method discussed in these notes can be used to construct a solution in a sufficiently small time interval without any assumption about the strength of the non-linearity.

We conclude this introduction with an outline of the remaining parts of these lecture notes:

•

In the next subsection, we illustrate how the renormalization problem becomes progressively more difficult as the order
\sigma
of the fractional Laplacian decreases. In particular, we discuss the proof of the main result in the Da Prato–Debussche regime, highlighting the key challenges that arise when extending the analysis to more singular regimes, which are the primary focus of these notes.

•

Section
2
introduces the flow equation approach in a systematic way. Here, the original SPDE is reformulated as an effective equation involving an effective force, and we state the analytic conditions ensuring well-posedness of this reformulation.

•

In Section
3
, we construct the effective force explicitly in terms of its effective force coefficients derived from the flow equation. We then define the enhanced noise as the finite collection of these coefficients and establish the corresponding stochastic estimates. These estimates are obtained by proving moment bounds and applying a Kolmogorov-type argument.

•

To control the enhanced noise, it is useful to study not only the moments of the effective force coefficients but also their joint cumulants. Section
4
introduces the notion of joint cumulants and derives the associated flow equation governing their evolution. This flow equation plays a role analogous to the Polchinski
[
Pol84
]
equation in quantum field theory.

•

In Section
5
, we use the flow equation to establish uniform bounds for the cumulants. The strength of the flow equation approach becomes particularly evident here: if the counterterms are chosen appropriately according to the boundary conditions of the flow equation, uniform bounds propagate automatically. This procedure eliminates the need for cumbersome, tree-based inductive arguments typically required in traditional renormalization proofs.

•

To complete the analysis, we derive pathwise estimates for the enhanced noise, viewed as the collection of effective force coefficients, using a generalization of the Kolmogorov continuity theorem, presented in Appendix
A
.

•

Appendix
B
collects auxiliary lemmas and technical results needed to finalize the proof of the main theorem. This appendix can be skipped by readers primarily interested in the conceptual aspects of the flow approach.

•

Finally, Appendix
C
contains a new result not previously discussed in the literature. It presents an alternative proof of the stochastic estimates, which bypasses the cumulant-based analysis developed in Sections
4
–
5
and Appendix
A
. This proof offers a different perspective on the underlying probabilistic estimates and can be read independently of the main argument.

Material marked with the symbol
(\spadesuit)
is of a technical nature and is not essential for understanding the core ideas. Exercises marked with
(\spadesuit)
are intended to fill in gaps in proofs rather than introduce new conceptual insights.

1.1
Da Prato-Debussche regime and beyond

Let us now examine how the analytic difficulty of the problem depends on the parameter
\sigma
. As
\sigma
decreases, the smoothing effect of the fractional Laplacian weakens, and new challenges emerge each time a critical regularity threshold is crossed.

We begin by outlining the proof of the main result in the simplest setting, commonly referred to as the Da Prato–Debussche regime, following the classical approach of Da Prato and Debussche
[
DPD03
]
. This regime serves as a conceptual baseline for understanding the limitations of classical techniques and for motivating the more sophisticated methods required in the more singular case, such as the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model.

We first recall some material on function spaces that quantify the regularity of functions and distributions. In order to measure regularity or irregularity, we use Hölder–Besov spaces
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\equiv B^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For positive, non-integer
\alpha
, this space coincides with the classical Hölder space.
For
\alpha\leq 0
, the space
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
consists of all distributions
\phi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\|\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|K_{\mu}\ast\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty,\qquad\qquad[\mu]:=\mu^{1/\sigma},

(1.8)

where
K_{\mu}(x):=[\mu]^{-d}K(x/[\mu])
,
\mu\in(0,1]
, for some non-negative
K\in C^{\lceil-\alpha\rceil}(\mathbb{M})
of fast decay that integrates to one. We say that
\phi\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
has regularity
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
if
\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let us recall several standard facts about Besov spaces (see, for instance,
[
BCD11
, Section 2.7–2.8]
):

(1)

If
\alpha+\beta>0
, then the pointwise product of test functions extends continuously as a map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\wedge\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(2)

The convolution with the Green function
G
, the fundamental solution of
(1-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}
, maps continuously
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(3)

For
\alpha\geq\beta
there is a continuous inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Furthermore, we note that sample paths of
\xi
, the periodisation of the white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, belong almost surely to the space
\mathscr{C}^{-d/2-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
\varepsilon>0
(see e.g.
[
MWX16
, Thm. 5]
). Consequently, the solution of (
1.6
) with
\kappa=0
and
\lambda=0
given by
G\ast\xi
belongs almost surely to the Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\alpha=\sigma-d/2-\varepsilon
. In the subcritical regime (
1.6
) with
\lambda\neq 0
is in some sense a small perturbation of the linear equation with
\lambda=0
. Consequently, we expect that the solution of (
1.6
) also lives in the Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 1.3
.

In what follows,
\alpha=\sigma-d/2-\varepsilon
denotes the expected regularity of the solution of (
1.6
) and
\varepsilon\in(0,\infty)
is assumed to be sufficiently small. Note that for smaller values of
\sigma
the equation is more singular as the regularizing effect of the Green function
G
is weaker.

Using the above-mentioned facts one easily shows that for
\sigma\in(d/2,\infty)
the equation we study,
\varPhi=G\ast(\xi-\lambda\,\varPhi^{3})
, is not singular and can be solved in a Hölder space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\alpha>0
using the Banach fixed-point theorem. In this regime no regularization is needed. Let us see what happens for smaller values of
\sigma
.

Lemma 1.4
.

The statement of Theorem
1.1
holds true for
\sigma\in(5d/12,d/2]
.

Sketch of the proof.

Recall that we want to solve the equation

\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast(\xi_{\kappa}+\lambda\,\varPhi_{\kappa}^{3}+\lambda\,c^{(1)}_{\kappa}\,\varPhi_{\kappa})

(1.9)

and prove the existence of the limit
\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\kappa}
. We have
G\ast\xi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and we expect that
\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. But since
\alpha<0
the cube
\varPhi_{0}^{3}
is classically ill-defined. We shall employ the so-called Da Prato–Debussche trick
[
DPD03
]
. Introducing the notation
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.90712pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure0.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}:=\xi_{\kappa}
and
x\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure1.pdf}}}}\,y\,:=G(x-y)
we define
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure2.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}=G\ast\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.90712pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure3.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}:=G\ast\xi_{\kappa}
and make the following ansatz for the solution of our equation

\varPhi_{\kappa}=\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure4.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+\varPsi_{\kappa}.

(1.10)

As we shall see, the remainder
\varPsi_{0}
has much better regularity than
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure5.pdf}}}}_{0},\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Using (
1.9
) one shows that
\varPsi_{\kappa}
satisfies the following equation

\varPsi_{\kappa}=\lambda\,G\ast(\varPsi_{\kappa}^{3}+3\varPsi_{\kappa}^{2}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure6.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+3\varPsi_{\kappa}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure7.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure8.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}),

(1.11)

where

\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure9.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}:=(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure10.pdf}}}}_{\kappa})^{2}+c^{(1)}_{\kappa}/3,\qquad\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure11.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}:=(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure12.pdf}}}}_{\kappa})^{3}+c^{(1)}_{\kappa}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure13.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\qquad c^{(1)}_{\kappa}:=-3\,\mathbb{E}\hskip 1.99168pt(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure14.pdf}}}}_{\kappa})^{2}

(1.12)

for all
\kappa\in(0,1]
.

The list of trees
(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure15.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure16.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure17.pdf}}}}_{\kappa})
is called the enhanced noise. Of course the products appearing in the definitions of the above trees are ill-defined deterministically for
\kappa=0
. However, the point is that the tress are simple and quite explicit objects. It turns out that it is possible to control the convergence of the enhanced noise as
\kappa\searrow 0
by studying its covariance. One shows that with the above choice of the counterterm the limit

\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure18.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure19.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure20.pdf}}}}_{\kappa})=:(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure21.pdf}}}}_{0},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure22.pdf}}}}_{0},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure23.pdf}}}}_{0})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(1.13)

exists almost surely and there exists a random variable
R\in[1,\infty]
such that
\mathbb{E}R^{n}<\infty
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and

\big\|\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure24.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\vee\big\|\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure25.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\vee\big\|\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure26.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq R

(1.14)

for all
\kappa\in[0,1]
. We call the above bounds the stochastic estimates for the enhanced noise.

To proceed we rewrite (
1.11
) as the fixed point equation of the map

\mathbf{Q}[\psi]\equiv\mathbf{Q}\big[\psi;\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure27.pdf}}}}\,,\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure28.pdf}}}},\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure29.pdf}}}}\,\big]:=\lambda\,G\ast\big(\psi^{3}+3\psi^{2}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure30.pdf}}}}+3\psi\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure31.pdf}}}}+\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure32.pdf}}}}\big).

(1.15)

We claim that the map

\mathbf{Q}:\,\>\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is well defined for all
(\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure33.pdf}}}},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure34.pdf}}}},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure35.pdf}}}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The above choice of the domain of
\mathbf{Q}
is dictated by the regularity of
G\ast\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure36.pdf}}}}\in\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Note that
3\alpha+\sigma>0
. Hence,
\psi
in the domain of
\mathbf{Q}
is a Hölder continuous function and the products

\psi^{2},\psi^{3}\in\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

are well defined. In order to make sure that the products

\psi^{2}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure37.pdf}}}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\qquad\psi\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure38.pdf}}}}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(1.16)

are well defined we have to check whether
(3\alpha+\sigma)+l\alpha>0
for
l\in\{1,2\}
. Since
\alpha<0
it is enough to check this for
l=2
. We obtain the condition
5\alpha+\sigma>0
, which is equivalent to
\sigma>5d/12
. Consequently, all of the products are well defined and belong to
\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. As a result, the map
\mathbf{Q}:\,\>\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}\to\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is well defined.
∎

Exercise 1.1
.

Complete the deterministic part of the above proof. Assume that the maps in Items (1), (2), (3) above have norms bounded by some constant
c\in[1,\infty)
. For
R\in[1,\infty)
let
\mathscr{B}_{R}
be the closed ball in
\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of radius

and let
\lambda_{\star}:=1/(100c^{3}R^{2})
. For all

let
\mathbf{Q}_{\kappa}[\psi]:=\mathbf{Q}[\psi;\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure39.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure40.pdf}}}}_{\kappa},\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure41.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}]
. Prove that for
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{\star},\lambda_{\star}]
and
\kappa\in[0,1]
the map

is well defined and is a contraction with the Lipschitz constant less than
1/2
. Conclude that for all
\kappa\in[0,1]
the map
\mathbf{Q}_{\kappa}
has a unique fixed point in
\mathscr{B}_{R}
denoted by
\varPsi_{\kappa}
. Next, using (
1.13
) show that for all
\psi\in\mathscr{B}_{R}
it holds
\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\mathbf{Q}_{\kappa}[\psi]=\mathbf{Q}_{0}[\psi]
. Conclude that
\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\varPsi_{\kappa}=\varPsi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{3\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\kappa}=\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The method of the above proof does not work for
\sigma\leq 5d/12
because the product of
\varPsi_{0}
and
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure42.pdf}}}}_{0}
is no longer well defined classically. The problem is that the Da Prato–Debussche remainder
\varPsi_{0}
does not have enough regularity. To proceed we apply the Da Prato–Debussche trick once again to remove the most singular tree
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure43.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}
on the RHS of (
1.11
). To this end, we make the following ansatz

\varPhi_{\kappa}=\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure44.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+\varPsi_{\kappa}=\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure45.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+\lambda\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure46.pdf}}}}_{\!\!\kappa}+\tilde{\varPsi}_{\kappa},\qquad\qquad\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure47.pdf}}}}_{\!\!\kappa}=G\ast\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure48.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}.

(1.17)

Eq. (
1.9
) implies that the remainder
\tilde{\varPsi}_{\kappa}
satisfies the equation

\tilde{\varPsi}_{\kappa}=\lambda\,G\ast\Big(\big(\tilde{\varPsi}_{\kappa}+\lambda\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure49.pdf}}}}_{\!\!\kappa})^{3}+3\big(\tilde{\varPsi}_{\kappa}+\lambda\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure50.pdf}}}}_{\!\!\kappa}\big)^{2}~\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure51.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+3\tilde{\varPsi}_{\kappa}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure52.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}+3\lambda~\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure53.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}\Big),

(1.18)

where the new tree is defined by

\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure54.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}:=\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure55.pdf}}}}_{\!\!\kappa}~\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure56.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}.

(1.19)

Suppose that
\sigma\in(2d/5,5d/12]
. Even though the product in the definition of the above tree is ill-defined deterministically no renormalization is necessary. One shows that

\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure57.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}=:\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure58.pdf}}}}_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

exists almost surely and there exists a random variable
R\in[1,\infty]
such that
\mathbb{E}R^{n}<\infty
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and

\big\|\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 3.0452pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure59.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq R

for all
\kappa\in[0,1]
.

Exercise 1.2

(
\spadesuit
)
.

Reformulate (
1.18
) as a fixed-point problem in a ball in
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and prove Theorem
1.1
for all
\sigma\in(2d/5,5d/12]
.

It turns out at
\sigma=2d/5
the Da Prato–Debussche method breaks down completely. Since
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure60.pdf}}}}_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\alpha=\sigma-d/2-\varepsilon
the term
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure61.pdf}}}}_{0}
, which cannot be removed using Da Prato–Debussche trick, can at best be defined as an element of
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. But then one can only hope that
G\ast(\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure62.pdf}}}}_{0})\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Thus, we should expect that
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. However, for
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha+\sigma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the product
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure63.pdf}}}}_{0}
is well defined only if
(2\alpha+\sigma)+2\alpha\geq 0
, which implies that
\sigma>2d/5
.

For
\sigma=2d/5
in order to control the product
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure64.pdf}}}}_{0}
and close the estimates more information about
\tilde{\varPsi}_{0}
is needed that cannot be quantified in terms of just the regularity. It turns out that writing an equation that is equivalent to the original equation (
1.6
) and makes sense in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
is quite challenging. We refer the interested reader to
[
Hai15
, Eq. (6.3), Prop. 7.5]
for the solution of the problem in the framework of regularity structures and to
[
MW17
, Section 1.1, 1.2]
or
[
JP23
, Thm. 2.1]
for the solution involving the decomposition of the products into the resonant term and the paraproducts. For smaller values of
\sigma
the problem becomes even more complicated. Let us mention that using the framework of regularity structures or the approach discussed in these notes one can study the full subcritical regime corresponding to
\sigma>d/3
. For
\sigma\leq d/3
the limit
\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure65.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
does not exist and consequently controlling the cubic term in the equation seems impossible.

In Section
2
we discuss a reformulation of (
1.6
) meaningful in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
in the flow equation framework. Let us only mention that the system of equations (
2.10
) that we will study does not involve the product
\varPhi_{\kappa}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure66.pdf}}}}_{\kappa}
at all. Instead, there is a term of the form
K_{\mu}\ast(\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.53802pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure67.pdf}}}}_{\kappa,\mu})
, where
K_{\mu}
is some regularizing kernel of characteristic length scale
[\mu]\in(0,1]
, the tree
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.53802pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure68.pdf}}}}_{\kappa,\mu}
is such that
\|K_{\mu}\ast\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.53802pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure69.pdf}}}}_{0,\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim[\mu]^{2\alpha-\varepsilon}
and
\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}
is the coarse-grained process capturing the behaviour of
\varPhi_{0}
at spatial scales larger than
[\mu]
. Because
\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}
is smooth at scales smaller than
[\mu]
the product
K_{\mu}\ast(\varPhi_{0,\mu}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.53802pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure70.pdf}}}}_{0,\mu})
is always well defined classically and the only non-trivial task is to control its norm uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
. Since we expect that
\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it should hold
\|\varPhi_{0,\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim[\mu]^{\alpha}
. Then it is possible to show that

\|K_{\mu}\ast(\varPhi_{0,\mu}\,\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.53802pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure71.pdf}}}}_{0,\mu})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim[\mu]^{3\alpha-\varepsilon},

(1.20)

which will turn out to be sufficient to close the estimates in full sub-critical regime. The fact that the tree
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.53802pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure72.pdf}}}}_{0,\mu}
depends on the scale
\mu
is not important. Note that the above bound would be also true for the standard tree
\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\immediate\hbox{\lower 1.33803pt\hbox{{\lxSVG@includegraphics{}{}{figures-ext/spde_flow_notes-figure73.pdf}}}}_{0}
. What is crucial is the fact that the system of equations (
2.10
) involves the coarse-grained process
\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}
instead of
\varPhi_{\kappa}
.

1.2
Literature

A general solution theory for singular SPDEs beyond the Da Prato–Debussche regime was first developed in
[
Hai14
]
, which introduced the framework of regularity structures. An alternative approach based on paracontrolled distributions was subsequently proposed in
[
GIP15
]
. The flow equation method, developed in
[
Duc25a
,
Duc25b
]
and discussed in these notes, was inspired by the renormalization group perspective
[
Wil71
]
on singular SPDEs formulated in
[
Kup16
,
KM17
]
. The main advantage of the flow equation formalism is its applicability to the entire subcritical regime, offering an alternative to the regularity structures framework developed in
[
CH16
,
BHZ19
,
BCCH21
,
HS24
]
and
[
OSSW25
,
LOTT24
]
.

These notes are primarily based on
[
Duc25a
,
Duc25b
]
, while also drawing on ideas from
[
DGR23
]
. In the latter work, the flow equation method was employed to construct a generalized
\Phi^{4}_{3}
model with a fractional Laplacian in the entire subcritical regime (see also
[
Duc24b
]
for a pedagogical exposition). Recent developments have extended the flow equation framework to encompass singular SPDEs with general non-polynomial nonlinearities, as well as rough differential equations
[
CF24a
,
CF24b
]
. Furthermore,
[
BM25
]
establishes that the renormalization scheme inherent in the flow equation formalism coincides with the BPHZ scheme of the regularity structures approach. For additional recent applications of the related Polchinski flow equation in the context of constructive quantum field theory, see
[
BB21
,
BBD24
,
GM24
,
Duc24a
,
DFG25
]
.

2
Effective equation

The starting point of the flow equation approach is a reformulation of the original SPDE (
1.6
) in terms of an effective equation, an equivalent description that captures how the nonlinear interactions evolve under progressive coarse-graining. The key advantage of this reformulation is that, unlike the original equation, it remains meaningful in the limit where the regularization is removed.

Definition 2.1
.

We use the notation
\mathbb{M}:=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{T}:=(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{d}
and
\|{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|:=\|{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{M})}
.

We would like to construct a solution
\varPhi_{\kappa}
of the equation

\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}],\qquad F_{\kappa}[\varphi]:=\xi_{\kappa}+\lambda\,\varphi^{3}+\sum_{i=1}^{i_{\sharp}}\lambda^{i}\,c_{\kappa}^{(i)}\varphi,

(2.1)

and prove that for an appropriate choice of the counterterms
(c_{\kappa}^{(i)})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,i_{\sharp}\}}
the limit

\lim_{\kappa\searrow 0}\varPhi_{\kappa}=:\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})

exists. Recall that
G\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
denotes the fundamental solution of the differential operator
(1-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}
. The functional
F_{\kappa}
is called the force. In the regime
\sigma\in(d/3,d/2]
, we are interested in, we expect that
\varPhi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
is not a function. Since multiplication of distributions is in general an ill-defined operation (
2.1
) becomes meaningless in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
. In this section we will formulate a certain system of equations such that under some assumptions: (1) for every
\kappa\in(0,1]
there is a correspondence between a solution of this system of equations and a solution of (
2.1
) and (2) the system of equations is well-posed in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
.

To this end, let us first introduce the notion of a scale decomposition of the Green function
G
and an effective force. A scale decomposition of
G
is a family of integrable kernels
G_{\mu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
parametrized by
\mu\in[0,1]
such that
G_{0}=G
,
G_{1}=0
and the map
[0,1]\ni\mu\mapsto G_{\mu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
is continuous and piecewise continuously differentiable with the derivative denoted by
\dot{G}_{\mu}
. Given the force functional
F_{\kappa}
an effective force is a family of functionals
F_{\kappa,\mu}\,:\,C(\mathbb{T})\to C(\mathbb{T})
parametrized by
\mu\in[0,1]
such that
F_{\kappa,0}=F_{\kappa}
and
[0,1]\ni\mu\mapsto F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]\in C(\mathbb{T})
is continuous and piecewise continuously differentiable for all
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{T})
. Moreover, we assume that
F_{\kappa,\mu}
is of polynomial type.

Definition 2.2
.

A functional of polynomial type is a map
V\,:\,C(\mathbb{T})\to C(\mathbb{T})
such that the directional derivatives of
V
at
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{T})
of order
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
along
\psi\in C(\mathbb{T})
, i.e.

\mathrm{D}^{k}V[\varphi]\cdot\psi^{\otimes k}:=\partial_{\tau}^{k}V[\varphi+\tau\psi]\big|_{\tau=0},

(2.2)

exist for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and are non-zero for only finitely many
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

In what follows, we will make a specific choice of a scale decomposition of the Green function and an effective force that is suitable for the problem at hand. For the time being, let us continue the informal discussion at a general level. Suppose that
\varPhi_{\kappa}
is a solution of the original equation
\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]
. For
\mu\in[0,1]
we define

\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}=G_{\mu}\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}].

(2.3)

We call
(\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu})_{\mu\in(0,1]}
the coarse-grained process. By our assumptions about
G_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}
we have
\varPhi_{\kappa,0}=\varPhi_{\kappa}
and
\varPhi_{\kappa,1}=0
. For
\mu\in[0,1]
the so-called remainder
\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}
is defined by the equation

F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]=F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]+\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}.

(2.4)

Because
F_{\kappa,0}=F_{\kappa}
and
\varPhi_{\kappa,0}=\varPhi_{\kappa}
we obtain
\zeta_{\kappa,0}=0
. Since the LHS of (
2.4
) does not depend on
\mu
it holds

\partial_{\mu}\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}=\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast(F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]+\zeta_{\kappa,\mu})

(2.5)

and

\partial_{\mu}\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}=-(\partial_{\mu}F_{\kappa,\mu})[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]-\mathrm{D}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]\cdot\partial_{\mu}\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}.

(2.6)

Plugging (
2.5
) into (
2.6
) we obtain

\partial_{\mu}\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}=-(\partial_{\mu}F_{\kappa,\mu})[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]-\mathrm{D}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]\cdot(\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast(F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]+\zeta_{\kappa,\mu})).

(2.7)

Using the notation

H_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]:=\partial_{\mu}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]+\mathrm{D}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]\cdot(\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi])

(2.8)

we rewrite (
2.7
) in the following way

\partial_{\mu}\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}=-H_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]-\mathrm{D}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}]\cdot(\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}).

(2.9)

Summing up, (
2.5
) and (
2.9
) together with the boundary conditions
\varPhi_{\kappa,1}=0
and
\zeta_{\kappa,0}=0
imply the following system of equations

\begin{cases}\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}=-\int_{\mu}^{1}\dot{G}_{\eta}\ast(F_{\kappa,\eta}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\eta}]+\zeta_{\kappa,\eta})\,\mathrm{d}\eta\\
\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}=-\int_{0}^{\mu}(H_{\kappa,\eta}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\eta}]+\mathrm{D}F_{\kappa,\eta}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\eta}]\cdot(\dot{G}_{\eta}\ast\zeta_{\kappa,\eta}))\,\mathrm{d}\eta\,.\end{cases}

(2.10)

The above system of equations for
(\varPhi_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}},\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}})
is called the
effective equation
. At a formal level, there is a one to one correspondence between solutions of the original equation
\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]
and the above system of equations. Given a solution
\varPhi_{\kappa}
of the equation
\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]
a solution
(\varPhi_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}},\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}})
of the above system of equations with
H_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
given by (
2.8
) is constructed as outlined above. On the other hand, given a solution
(\varPhi_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}},\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}})
of the above system of equations with
H_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
given by (
2.8
) one shows that (
2.4
) is satisfied and concludes that
\varPhi_{\kappa}=\varPhi_{\kappa,0}
solves the equation
\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]
. The advantage of the system of equations (
2.10
) is that, as we will see later, for an appropriate choice of an effective force
F_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
it remains meaningful in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
.

Remark 2.3
.

In most practical situations the framework discussed above is useful only if the scale decomposition of
G
is chosen in such a way that for all
\mu\in(0,1]
the kernel
G_{\mu}
is smooth (or has sufficiently many derivatives) and
G_{\mu}
is essentially constant at spatial scales smaller than
[\mu]=\mu^{1/\sigma}
. For such choices of the scale decomposition of
G
we expect that the coarse grained process
\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}
captures the behaviour of
\varPhi_{\kappa}
, the solution of
\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]
, at spatial scales larger than
[\mu]=\mu^{1/\sigma}
and is essentially constant at smaller scales. Informally, you can imagine that
\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}
is obtained by averaging
\varPhi_{\kappa}
over blocks of size
[\mu]
. In particular,
\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}
is smooth for every
\mu>0
. Moreover, at least morally, we expect that

\|\varPhi_{\kappa}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{M})}:=\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|K_{\mu}\ast\varPhi_{\kappa}\|\simeq\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}\|

(2.11)

for all
\alpha<\sigma-d/2
and we hope to bound the above Besov norm uniformly in
\kappa\in(0,1]
.

Remark 2.4
.

A possible choice for an effective force
F_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
, made in
[
Duc25a
,
Duc25b
]
as well as in
[
Kup16
,
KM17
]
, is to define it in such a way that
H_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
given by (
2.8
) vanishes identically. An effective force satisfies then the so-called flow equation

\partial_{\mu}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]+\mathrm{D}F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]\cdot(\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast F_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi])=0.

(2.12)

In a situations in which a small parameter is available solving the above equation is usually unproblematic. However, the solution is not a functional of polynomial type. Since the equation for the remainder
\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
in the system (
2.10
) is linear and
H_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}=0
the unique solution is given by
\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}=0
. Consequently,
\varPhi_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
satisfies the following effective equation

\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}=-\int_{\mu}^{1}\dot{G}_{\eta}\ast F_{\kappa,\eta}[\varPhi_{\kappa,\eta}]\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

(2.13)

The advantage of the formulation involving
(\varPhi_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}},\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}})
, proposed in
[
DGR23
]
, is that there is more flexibility in the choice of an effective force
F_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
as it has to satisfy the flow equation only up to some error term
H_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
. In particular, a suitable effective force can usually be constructed without exploiting the presence of a small parameter, even though a small parameter is typically needed anyway to solve (
2.10
).

In order to prove well-posedness of the system of equations (
2.10
) an effective force
F_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}}
has to satisfy some additional conditions, which we shall formulate below. To this end, let us first introduce some regularizing kernels and fix a convenient scale decomposition of the Green function.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{M})
be the space of signed measures on
\mathbb{M}
with finite total variation. We set
\|K\|_{\mathcal{K}}=\int_{\mathbb{M}}|K(\mathrm{d}x)|
. For
x\in\mathbb{M}
we denote by
\delta_{x}\in\mathcal{K}
the Dirac delta at
x
.

Remark 2.6
.

It holds
\|\delta_{x}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
and
\|K\|_{\mathcal{K}}=\|K\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{M})}
for all
K\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})\subset\mathcal{K}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mu\in[0,1]
and
[\mu]=\mu^{1/\sigma}
. The kernel
\tilde{K}_{\mu}\in\mathcal{K}
is the unique solution of
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}\tilde{K}_{\mu}=\delta_{0}
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}:=(1-[\mu]^{2}\Delta)^{d+2}
. We define
K_{\mu}:=\tilde{K}_{\mu}\ast\tilde{K}_{\mu}\ast\tilde{K}_{\mu}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}:=\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}^{3}
.

The kernels
K_{\mu},\tilde{K}_{\mu}
are of exponential decay with the characteristic length scale
[\mu]
. The kernels
K_{\mu},\tilde{K}_{\mu}
are not smooth. However, for
\mu\in(0,1]
the convolution of a distribution with
K_{\mu}
or
\tilde{K}_{\mu}
has some smoothing effect. In what follows, we often use the fact
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}K_{\mu}=\delta_{0}
to introduce a regularizing kernel where it is needed, e.g.
\psi\ast\phi=(\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\psi)\ast(K_{\mu}\ast\phi)
. We will frequently use the properties of the regularizing kernels stated in the exercise below.

Exercise 2.1
.

Prove the following statements:

(1)

\tilde{K}_{0}=\delta_{0}
and
\tilde{K}_{\mu}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})\cap C^{d}_{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbb{M})
for
\mu\in(0,1]
.

(2)

For all
\mu\in[0,1]
the kernel
\tilde{K}_{\mu}
is a positive measure and
\|\tilde{K}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
.

(3)

For all
0<\eta\leq\mu\leq 1
there exist
\tilde{K}_{\mu,\eta}\in\mathcal{K}
such that
\|\tilde{K}_{\mu,\eta}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=1
and
\tilde{K}_{\mu}=\tilde{K}_{\mu,\eta}\ast\tilde{K}_{\eta}
.

Conclude that the kernels
K_{\mu}
,
\mu\in[0,1]
, also have the above properties.
Hint for Item (3): Let
\hat{K}_{\mu}\in\mathcal{K}
be the solution of
\hat{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}\hat{K}_{\mu}=\delta_{0}
, where
\hat{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}:=(1-[\mu]^{2}\Delta)
. It holds
\hat{K}_{\mu}=\hat{K}_{\mu,\eta}\ast\hat{K}_{\eta}
for
\hat{K}_{\mu,\eta}=[\eta/\mu]^{2}\,\delta_{0}+(1-[\eta/\mu]^{2})\,\hat{K}_{\mu}\in\mathcal{K}
.

Remark 2.8
.

Recall that
G\in L^{1}(\mathbb{M})
is the fundamental solution for the pseudo-differential operator
(1-\Delta)^{\sigma/2}
. Note that
G
is smooth outside the origin. For every multi-index
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
it holds
|\partial^{a}G(x)|\lesssim|x|^{\sigma-d-|a|}
uniformly for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
. Furthermore,
\partial^{a}G
is of fast decay at infinity for every
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
.

Definition 2.9

(Scale decomposition of
G
)
.

Fix
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\chi(r)=0
for
|r|\leq 1/4
and
\chi(r)=1
for
|r|\geq 1/2
and let
\chi_{\mu}(r):=\chi(r(1-\mu)/\mu)
for
\mu\in(0,1]
. For
\mu\in(0,1]
the smooth kernels
G_{\mu},\dot{G}_{\mu}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{M})
are defined by

G_{\mu}(x):=\chi_{\mu}(|x|^{\sigma})\,G(x),\qquad\dot{G}_{\mu}:=\partial_{\mu}G_{\mu}.

(2.14)

Remark 2.10
.

Note that we chose to work with the scale decomposition of
G
in position space. An alternative would be to introduce the decomposition of
G
in Fourier space. The advantage of the position space decomposition is that the kernel
\dot{G}_{\mu}
is supported in a shell
\{x\in\mathbb{M}\,|\,\mu/4<(1-\mu)|x|^{\sigma}<\mu/2\}
. More specifically, it will play an important role that
\mathrm{supp}\,\dot{G}_{\mu}\subset\{x\in\mathbb{M}\,|\,|x|\leq[\mu]\}
for
\mu\in(0,1/2]
. The above support property will allow us to completely avoid using weights in the subsequent analysis. The price to pay is that the last equality in (
2.11
) is not obvious and a little extra argument, which is part of Exercise
B.2
, is needed to control the convergence in a Besov norm. The latter fact would be obvious in the case of the Fourier space decomposition of
G
but then one would have to work with weighted spaces, which makes some estimates look more complicated.

Lemma 2.11
.

For all
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
it holds
\|\tilde{\mathbf{P}}^{l}_{\mu}\dot{G}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim 1
uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
.

Proof
\mathrm{(}\spadesuit\mathrm{)}
.

First note that
\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\mu}(|x|^{\sigma})
vanishes unless
\mu/4<(1-\mu)|x|^{\sigma}\leq\mu/2
. Moreover, for all
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
we have

|\partial^{a}\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\mu}(|x|^{\sigma})|\lesssim|x|^{\sigma-|a|}/\mu^{2}

(2.15)

uniformly in
\mu\in(0,1]
and
x\in\mathbb{M}
. Using the properties of the kernel
G
mentioned in Remark
2.8
we obtain
\|\partial^{a}\dot{G}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}\lesssim[\mu]^{-|a|}
for all
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
. This implies the lemma since
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\mu}=(1-[\mu]^{2}\Delta)^{d+2}
.
∎

In order to make sense of the system of equations (
2.10
) in the limit
\kappa\searrow 0
we shall rewrite it in such a way that it involves only the regularized functionals

\tilde{F}_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]:=K_{\mu}\ast F_{\kappa,\mu}[K_{\mu}\ast\varphi],\qquad\tilde{H}_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]:=K_{\mu}\ast H_{\kappa,\mu}[K_{\mu}\ast\varphi].

(2.16)

Note that the above functionals are obtained by convolving the original functionals at scale
\mu
with the regularizing kernel
K_{\mu}
at the same scale. At a heuristic level, the system of equations (
2.10
) is equivalent to

\begin{cases}\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\mu}=-\int_{\mu}^{1}K_{\eta,\mu}\ast\tilde{G}_{\eta}\ast(\tilde{F}_{\kappa,\eta}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\eta}]+\tilde{\zeta}_{\kappa,\eta})\,\mathrm{d}\eta\\
\tilde{\zeta}_{\kappa,\mu}=-\int_{0}^{\mu}K_{\mu,\eta}\ast(\tilde{H}_{\kappa,\eta}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\eta}]+\mathrm{D}\tilde{F}_{\kappa,\eta}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\eta}]\cdot(\tilde{G}_{\eta}\ast\tilde{\zeta}_{\kappa,\eta}))\,\mathrm{d}\eta\end{cases}

(2.17)

where
\tilde{G}_{\mu}:=\mathbf{P}_{\mu}^{2}\dot{G}_{\mu}
and the kernels
(K_{\mu,\eta})_{0\leq\eta\leq\mu\leq 1}
were introduced in Exercise
2.1
. Note that
(\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}},\tilde{\zeta}_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}})
is related to
(\varPhi_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}},\zeta_{\kappa,{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}})
appearing in the system of equations (
2.10
) by

\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\mu}=\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu},\qquad\tilde{\zeta}_{\kappa,\mu}=K_{\mu}\ast\zeta_{\kappa,\mu}.

(2.18)

Remark 2.12

(
\spadesuit
)
.

Formally, the equivalence of the systems of equations (
2.10
) and (
2.17
) is an immediate consequence of the properties of the regularizing kernel
K_{\mu}
. More specifically, we use the following identities

\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}=K_{\mu}\ast\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\varPhi_{\kappa,\mu}=K_{\mu}\ast\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\mu},\qquad\dot{G}_{\mu}=K_{\mu}\ast\mathbf{P}_{\mu}^{2}\dot{G}_{\mu}\ast K_{\mu}=K_{\mu}\ast\tilde{G}_{\mu}\ast K_{\mu}

(2.19)

as well as
K_{\mu}=K_{\mu,\eta}\ast K_{\eta}
for
\mu\geq\eta
and
\mathbf{P}_{\mu}\dot{G}_{\eta}=K_{\eta,\mu}\ast\tilde{G}_{\eta}\ast K_{\eta}
for
\eta\geq\mu
.

Remark 2.13
.

Note that
\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}\|\tilde{G}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}=:C_{G}<\infty
by Lemma
2.11
and
\|K_{\eta,\mu}\|=1
for all
0\leq\eta\leq\mu\leq 1
by Exercise
2.1
.

Thanks to the presence of the regularizing kernels in the definition of
\tilde{F}_{\kappa,\mu}
and
\tilde{H}_{\kappa,\mu}
we will be able to control the limit of these functionals as
\kappa\searrow 0
. For the choice of an effective force, which will be specified in the next section,
F_{\kappa,\mu}
is in some sense a small perturbation of the noise
\xi_{\kappa}
. Hence,
\tilde{F}_{\kappa,\mu}
is in some sense a small perturbation
K_{\mu}\ast\xi_{\kappa}
. Since the bound
\|K_{\mu}\ast\xi_{\kappa}\|\lesssim\mu^{\alpha-\sigma}
uniform in
\kappa,\mu\in(0,1]
is satisfied almost surely for all
\alpha<\sigma-d/2\leq 0
we expect a bound of the form
\|\tilde{F}_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]\|\lesssim[\mu]^{\alpha-\sigma}
uniform in
\kappa,\mu\in(0,1]
for arbitrary fixed
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{T})
and all
\alpha<\sigma-d/2
. Then by the first of the equations (
2.17
) we can hope that

\|\tilde{\varPhi}_{\kappa,\mu}\|\lesssim\int_{\mu}^{1}[\eta]^{\alpha-\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\lesssim[\mu]^{\alpha}

(2.20)

uniformly in
\kappa,\mu\in(0,1]
, which, as we argued in Remark
2.3
, is consistent with the fact that
\|\varPhi_{\kappa}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{M})}
should be uniformly bounded in
\kappa\in(0,1]
. In order to make sense of the second of the equations (
2.17
) we need a bound of the form
\|\tilde{H}_{\kappa,\mu}[\varphi]\|\lesssim[\mu]^{\beta-\sigma}
uniform in
\kappa,\mu\in(0,1]
for arbitrary fixed
\varphi\in C(\mathbb{T})
and some
\beta>0
. Then

\|\tilde{\zeta}_{\kappa,\mu}\|\lesssim\int_{0}^{\mu}[\eta]^{\beta-\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\eta\lesssim[\mu]^{\beta}.

(2.21)

We stress that it is crucial that
\beta>0
for the above bound to be valid.

Definition 2.14
.

For
\alpha\in(-\infty,0)
,
\beta\in(0,\infty)
and
R\in[1,\infty)
we define
\mathscr{B}_{R}
to be the set of continuous maps

(0,1]\ni\mu\mapsto(\tilde{\varPhi}_{\mu},\tilde{\zeta}_{\mu})\in C(\mathbb{T})\times C(\mathbb{T})

(2.22)

such that

\|(\tilde{\varPhi}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}},\tilde{\zeta}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}})\|_{\mathscr{B}_{R}}:=\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}\,[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\tilde{\varPhi}_{\mu}\|+R\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}\,[\mu]^{-\beta}\,\|\tilde{\zeta}_{\mu}\|\leq R^{2}.

(2.23)

Lemma 2.15
.

Fix
\alpha\in(-\infty,0)
,
\beta\in(0,\infty)
and
R\in[1,\infty)
such that

R\,(|\alpha|\wedge\beta)>100\,\sigma\,(C_{G}\vee 1),\qquad\qquad C_{G}:=\sup_{\mu\in(0,1]}\|\tilde{G}_{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{K}}<\infty.

(2.24)

Suppose that

(\tilde{F}_{\mu})_{\mu\in(0,1]},\qquad\qquad(\tilde{H}_{\mu})_{\mu\in(0,1]}

are families of functionals of polynomial type depending continuously on
\mu\in(0,1]
such that for some
m_{\flat}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
it holds

[\mu]^{\sigma-\alpha}\,\|\mathrm{D}^{k}\tilde{F}_{\mu}[\varphi]\cdot\psi^{\otimes k}\,\|\leq R\,(|\lambda|^{1/3}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\psi\|)^{k}\,(1/2+|\lambda|^{1/3}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\varphi\|)^{m_{\flat}},

(2.25)

[\mu]^{\sigma-\beta}\,\|\mathrm{D}^{k}\tilde{H}_{\mu}[\varphi]\cdot\psi^{\otimes k}\,\|\leq|\lambda|^{1/3}\,R^{2}\,(|\lambda|^{1/3}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\psi\|)^{k}\,(1/2+|\lambda|^{1/3}[\mu]^{-\alpha}\,\|\varphi\|)^{m_{\flat}}

(2.26)

for all
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,
\mu\in(0,1]
,
\varphi,\psi\in C(\mathbb{T})
and
\lambda\in[-1,1]
. Let
\lambda_{\star}:=1/(2R^{2})^{3}
and suppose that
\lambda\in[-\lambda_{\star},\lambda_{\star}]
. Under the above assumptions the map
\mathbf{Q}\,:\,\mathscr{B}_{R}\to\mathscr{B}_{R}
,

\mathbf{Q}\big[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}},\tilde{\zeta}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\displaystyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\textstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{\hskip 1.42262pt$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$\hskip 1.42262pt}}}}}}\big]:=\begin{pmatrix}\mu\mapsto-\int_{\mu}^{1}K_{\eta,\mu}\ast\tilde{G}_{\eta}\ast(\tilde{F}_{\eta}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\eta}]+\tilde{\zeta}_{\eta})\,\mathrm{d}\eta\\
\mu\mapsto-\int_{0}^{\mu}K_{\mu,\eta}\ast(\tilde{H}_{\eta}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\eta}]+\mathrm{D}\tilde{F}_{\eta}[\tilde{\varPhi}_{\eta}]\cdot(\tilde{G}_{\eta}\ast\tilde{\zeta}_{\eta}))\,\mathrm{d}\eta\end{pmatrix}\,,

(2.27)

is well defined and is a contraction with the Lipschitz constant less than
1/2
.

Remark 2.16
.

Let us explain the relation between the fixed point equation for the map
\mathbf{Q}
and the original equation,
\varPhi_{\kappa}=G\ast F_{\kappa}[\varPhi_{\kappa}]
. Of course, in general, there is no relation at all. However, assume that:

(1)
